# React UX Aspects Documentation

Generated: 2026-03-31T04:14:12.584Z

---

This site provides documentation for JATO UX (OpenText Common User Experience Version 3.0) components. For details on Micro Focus components, please visit React storybook.

UX Aspects Universal is a UI component library with a focus on accessibility, customization, and developer experience.
Whether you're building an application from scratch or looking to enhance an existing one, our components are here to
streamline the process and boost your productivity.

## Why choose Universal?

1. **Architected for the future:** Universal was designed from the ground up using the latest browser features and best practices. We support the latest versions of each framework and provide automated migrations to keep your code up to date.

2. **Dynamic theming:** Universal components leverage CSS variables allowing every aspect of the components to change in real time. All components support both light and dark themes out of the box and support development of custom themes should you require them.

3. **Accelerate development:** Developing components that can be used across your application is a time consuming process, especially when branding guidelines are updated. With Universal the components are already at your disposal and are automatically updated as designs change, keeping your application up to date.

4. **Incremental adoption:** Universal is designed to be incrementally adoptable. It will work alongside any existing styling you have in your application. Components styles are encapsulated to prevent them leaking out and causing unexpected styling changes to other parts of your application.

5. **Accessible and localisable:** All of our components have been designed to meet WCAG AA 2.2 accessibility guidelines to ensure they cater for everyone. All components support both left-to-right and right-to-left languages and contain no hard coded strings.

6. **Rapid assistance:** Join the Universal MS Teams channel where you have instant access to our team of experienced web developers who are on hand to answer any questions you have, or resolve any issues in a timely manner.

---

This is the React quickstart guide for UX Aspects Universal. This guide will help you get started with Universal in your React application.

### Registry Configuration

Configure your package manager to use the company NPM registry by adding the following to a configuration file in the root of your project:

### npm

```bash
# .npmrc
@ux-aspects-universal:registry=https://artifactory.otxlab.net/artifactory/api/npm/uxa-universal-npm-rel/
```

### yarn 1.22

```bash
# .yarnrc
"@ux-aspects-universal:registry" "https://artifactory.otxlab.net/artifactory/api/npm/uxa-universal-npm-rel/"
```

### yarn 2+

```bash
# .yarnrc.yml
npmScopes:
  ux-aspects-universal:
    npmRegistryServer: 'https://artifactory.otxlab.net/artifactory/api/npm/uxa-universal-npm-rel/'
```

### Install Packages

### npm

```bash
npm i @ux-aspects-universal/react @ux-aspects-universal/themes
npm install @ux-aspects-universal/cli --save-dev
```

### yarn

```bash
yarn add @ux-aspects-universal/react @ux-aspects-universal/themes
yarn add @ux-aspects-universal/cli -D
```

If you are using NPM v7+, peer dependencies will be installed automatically. If you are using Yarn, or NPM v6 or lower, then the following packages need to be installed manually:

### npm

```bash
npm install @floating-ui/react clsx prop-types react-aria react-stately
```

### yarn

```bash
yarn add @floating-ui/react clsx prop-types react-aria react-stately
```

### Theming

Universal ships with a number of themes that can be used in your application. Theming can be configured in one of two ways:

If your application will only use a single theme, you can import the theme's stylesheet into your global stylesheet. For example, to use the Jato theme, add the following to your global stylesheet:

```css
@import '~@ux-aspects-universal/themes/jato/index.css';
```

Additionally you will need to define the active theme as a provider:

```tsx
import { ThemeProvider, Theme } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/theming';

render(
  <ThemeProvider theme={Theme.JatoLight}>
    <App />
  </ThemeProvider>
);
```

If your application will support multiple themes, they can be lazy loaded.

```tsx
import { ThemeProvider, ThemeStylesheets, Theme } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/theming';

const stylesheets: ThemeStylesheets = {
  [Theme.JatoLight]: () => import('@ux-aspects-universal/themes/jato').then(m => m.default),
  [Theme.JatoDark]: () => import('@ux-aspects-universal/themes/jato').then(m => m.default),
};
render(
  <ThemeProvider theme={Theme.JatoLight} stylesheets={stylesheets}>
    <App />
  </ThemeProvider>
);
```

### Typography

Universal supports both Inter and Roboto font families. The Inter font family should be used in applications that are using the Jato theme, while the Roboto font family should be used in applications that are using the legacy Micro Focus/OpenText Stage 1 themes.

We recommend installing the appropriate NPM packages to use the respective font families:

- [Inter](https://fonts.google.com/specimen/Inter): `@fontsource-variable/inter`
- [Roboto](https://fonts.google.com/specimen/Roboto): `@fontsource/roboto`

Additionally, if your application needs to support additional languages the following fonts are recommended:

- [Japanese](https://fonts.google.com/specimen/Noto+Sans+JP): `@fontsource/noto-sans-jp`
- [Chinese](https://fonts.google.com/specimen/Noto+Sans+SC): `@fontsource/noto-sans-sc`
- [Korean](https://fonts.google.com/specimen/Noto+Sans+KR): `@fontsource/noto-sans-kr`

In addition to installing the fonts, you will also need to import the font stylesheets into your global stylesheet, for example:

### Inter

```css
@import '@fontsource-variable/inter/index.css';
```

### Roboto

```css
@import '@fontsource/roboto/index.css';
```

You also need to inform Universal which font family you are using.

### Inter

```tsx
import { ThemeProvider, Theme, ThemeFeature } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/theming';

render(
  <ThemeProvider theme={Theme.JatoLight} features={[ThemeFeature.InterFont]}>
    <App />
  </ThemeProvider>
);
```

### Roboto

```tsx
import { ThemeProvider, Theme, ThemeFeature } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/theming';

render(
  <ThemeProvider theme={Theme.JatoLight} features={[ThemeFeature.RobotoFont]}>
    <App />
  </ThemeProvider>
);
```

#### Additional Languages

You can set up your application to use these fonts by adding the following to your global stylesheet:

```css
*:lang(ja) {
  --uxa-typography__font-family: var(--uxa-typography__font-family--ja);
  font-family: var(--uxa-typography__font-family);
}

*:lang(ko) {
  --uxa-typography__font-family: var(--uxa-typography__font-family--ko);
  font-family: var(--uxa-typography__font-family);
}

*:lang(zh-Hans),
*:lang(zh-CN) {
  --uxa-typography__font-family: var(--uxa-typography__font-family--zh-hans);
  font-family: var(--uxa-typography__font-family);
}
```

### Date time adapter

**You must configure a date time adapter before using any date or time related components.** Universal supports multiple date and time libraries through an adapter pattern that allows components to work with any date library without being tightly coupled to a specific implementation.

We recommend using Luxon for most applications. Install the required package:

### npm

```bash
npm install luxon
```

### yarn

```bash
yarn add luxon
```

Then configure the adapter by wrapping your application with `DateTimeAdapterContext.Provider`:

```tsx
import { UxaLuxonDateTimeAdapter } from '@ux-aspects-universal/core/date-time-luxon';
import { DateTimeAdapterContext } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/date-time';
import { StrictMode } from 'react';
import { createRoot } from 'react-dom/client';
import { App } from './app';

const luxonAdapter = new UxaLuxonDateTimeAdapter();

createRoot(document.getElementById('root')!).render(
  <StrictMode>
    <DateTimeAdapterContext.Provider value={luxonAdapter}>
      <App />
    </DateTimeAdapterContext.Provider>
  </StrictMode>
);
```

For more information about date time adapters, including alternative adapters (Moment.js, Native Date) and internationalization options, see the [Date time adapters](/react/get-started/date-time-adapters) guide.

### Encapsulation

If you are integrating UX Aspects Universal into an existing app, you can import our encapsulated components.
This will modify the CSS to ensure that application styles do not affect the component's styles.

```tsx
import { Alert } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/encapsulated/alert';

function MyComponent() {
  return <Alert status="info">This is an alert.</Alert>;
}
```

### Styled components

> **Note:** The Styled Components library is now in [maintenance mode](https://opencollective.com/styled-components/updates/thank-you) so it is no longer recommended to use it.

UX Aspects Universal provides an interop layer between styled components the Universal theming system.

If you are using styled components, you can use the `ThemeProvider` from `@ux-aspects-universal/react/styled-components` to
expose Universal's theme to your styled components.

```tsx
import { ThemeProvider, Theme } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/styled-components';
import { jatoLight } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/styled-components/jato-light';

render(
  <ThemeProvider theme={Theme.JatoLight} themeProps={jatoLight}>
    <App />
  </ThemeProvider>
);
```

The `themeProps` can be omitted and the theme will be lazy loaded.

Should you wish to modify any theme properties or add your own custom theme properties, the `ThemeProvider` has an `overrides` prop which can be passed an object with the desired theme values.

---

Composability is the practice of building complex user interfaces from smaller, self-contained and reusable components. It promotes flexibility, adaptability and maintainability by allowing developers to create applications using modular building blocks. In UX Aspects Universal, this approach supports scalable UI development through modular design.

Consider this concept like Lego blocks. Each component serves a specific purpose and can be easily combined or rearranged. This allows you to break down large software into smaller, indepedendent parts. You can quickly build new systems without starting from scratch, accelerating development and maximizing existing assets.

## Nesting & combining components

Components in UX Aspects Universal are designed to work seamlessly together. For example, you might place an avatar inside a card or include an icon within a navigation tab. This makes it easy to build rich, interactive interfaces while keeping your codebase clean and organized.

## Code example

```tsx
import { Button } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/button';
import { Form, FormActionGroup, FormField, FormFieldLabel } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/form';
import {
  Table,
  TableBody,
  TableCell,
  TableHead,
  TableHeaderCell,
  TableRow,
} from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/table';
import { Tab, TabGroup } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/tabs';
import { TextInput } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/text-input';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const ComposabilityExample: FC = () => {
  return (
    <TabGroup style={{ width: '90%' }}>
      <Tab label="Form">
        <Form style={{ width: '100%', marginBlock: '1rem' }}>
          <FormField>
            <FormFieldLabel isRequired>Name</FormFieldLabel>
            <TextInput placeholder="Enter full name" type="text" />
          </FormField>
          <FormActionGroup>
            <Button primary type="submit">
              Submit
            </Button>
          </FormActionGroup>
        </Form>
      </Tab>

      <Tab label="Table">
        <Table style={{ width: '100%', marginBlock: '1rem' }}>
          <TableHead>
            <TableRow>
              <TableHeaderCell>Header A</TableHeaderCell>
              <TableHeaderCell>Header B</TableHeaderCell>
              <TableHeaderCell>Header C</TableHeaderCell>
            </TableRow>
          </TableHead>
          <TableBody>
            <TableRow>
              <TableCell>Row 1A</TableCell>
              <TableCell>Row 1B</TableCell>
              <TableCell>Row 1C</TableCell>
            </TableRow>
            <TableRow>
              <TableCell>Row 2A</TableCell>
              <TableCell>Row 2B</TableCell>
              <TableCell>Row 2C</TableCell>
            </TableRow>
          </TableBody>
        </Table>
      </Tab>
    </TabGroup>
  );
};

export default ComposabilityExample;
```

---

Accessibility is a foundational requirement of the new JATO user experience, and the UX Design team has considered accessibility in every aspect of JATO – from headers and navigation, to colors, iconography, notifications, and the rest of the design. The JATO design includes essential accessible elements like contrast ratios and font choices that will benefit users by providing a strong, inclusive experience.

However, for the final product to be truly accessible, the JATO design elements must be implemented using coding practices that align with the Web Content Accessibility Guidelines (WCAG v2.2). Developers need to ensure that all accessibility requirements function seamlessly (e.g. keyboard-only navigation, proper tab order, screen reader compatibility, and screen magnification etc.). Test teams must then validate these implementations against WCAG criteria to guarantee that users with disabilities can interact with the product without barriers.

Achieving true JATO accessibility requires collaboration: an accessible product and UX design, implemented with accessible coding practices and verified by skilled testing. Only with these combined efforts will we be able to deliver a truly inclusive product.

## UX Aspects Universal and Accessibility

While the components that UX Aspects Universal provide are built with accessibility in mind, there are things that you as an application developer can do to make the experience cohesive and useable for all users.

UX Aspects Universal components are designed and developed not to be everything for everyone. By giving you the ability to define additional attributes like Accessible Rich Internet Applications (ARIA) you can develop experiences relevant and meaningful to your users.

Below are some examples of code you can reference. It is also recommended that you refer to the ARIA (Accessible Rich Internet Applications) documentation to help guide you in your implementation. For most of the UX Aspect Universal components you can find accessibility input controls listed on the corresponding documentation page.

## Accessibility in HTML

In HTML, accessibility means structuring and coding content so that it’s usable for all, including people with disabilities. This involves using semantic tags and ARIA attributes to convey the purpose and state of elements to assistive technologies like screen readers. Accessible HTML ensures that users can navigate, understand, and interact with content using keyboards, voice commands, and other aids, aligning with standards like WCAG to create inclusive web experiences.

## Recommended tools, extensions and links for reference

To help you in your accessibility development, here is a collection of recommended tools, IDE and browser extensions and links to documentation for references.

- [Open Text UXD - Accessibility Fundamentals](https://uxd.opentext.net/fundamentals/accessibility.htm)
- [W3 - Web Content Accessibility Guidelines (WCAG)](https://www.w3.org/WAI/standards-guidelines/wcag/)
- [MDN Web Docs - ARIA](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/Accessibility/ARIA)
- [Google Chrome Extension - taba11y](https://chrome.google.com/webstore/detail/taba11y/aocppmckdocdjkphmofnklcjhdidgmga)
- [Google Web Store - Google Screen Reader extension](https://chromewebstore.google.com/detail/screen-reader/kgejglhpjiefppelpmljglcjbhoiplfn)

## Code examples

### Global header

The UX Aspects Universal `@ux-aspects-universal/react/masthead` package contains composable components that allow you to build the **Global Header** pattern used at the top of applications with branding, user profile, search or global actions.

Following the documentation samples from the component page, the example implementation for a Global header provides the logo, product identity and access to global features shown below:

```tsx
import { Avatar } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/avatar';
import { IconButton } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/button';
import {
  AviatorPrimaryIcon,
  HelpCircleIcon,
  MenuIcon,
  MoreCircleIcon,
  NotificationIcon,
  SearchIcon,
  StarIcon,
} from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/icon/jato';
import { OpentextSmallLogo } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/logo';
import {
  Masthead,
  MastheadTitle,
  MastheadTitleSecondary,
  MastheadVersion,
  MyAviatorButton,
} from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/masthead';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const GlobalHeaderExample: FC = () => (
  <Masthead
    logo={<OpentextSmallLogo />}
    menuButton={<IconButton flat primary icon={<MenuIcon />} aria-label="menu button" />}
    title={
      <MastheadTitle>
        Primary name
        <MastheadTitleSecondary>Secondary name</MastheadTitleSecondary>
        <MastheadVersion>24.1</MastheadVersion>
      </MastheadTitle>
    }
    actions={[
      <IconButton key="search" flat icon={<SearchIcon />} aria-label="action" />,
      <IconButton key="aviator" flat icon={<AviatorPrimaryIcon />} aria-label="action" />,
      <IconButton key="notification" flat icon={<NotificationIcon />} aria-label="action" />,
      <IconButton key="star" flat icon={<StarIcon />} aria-label="action" />,
      <IconButton key="help" flat icon={<HelpCircleIcon />} aria-label="action" />,
      <IconButton key="more" flat icon={<MoreCircleIcon />} aria-label="action" />,
    ]}
    avatar={<Avatar initials="AA" />}
    myAviator={<MyAviatorButton>MyAviator</MyAviatorButton>}
  />
);

export default GlobalHeaderExample;
```

After adding the code within your project, you will see the **Global header** visible as per the example. By default it will have Keyboard navigation set up within its bounds. To test this, you can place your cursor or focus in the browser address bar then press the `Tab` key on your keyboard. You should see a focus ring around the menu button. If you were a user who had vision issues you would likely have a screen reader enabled. Upon focusing on the button it would describe the button as `Button`. This on its own is not descriptive enough to know which button, or what the action performed would be, if activated.

Pressing `Tab` again will show the focus ring leaving the menu button and onto the next focusable element in our case the `Search` button. Again like before the screen reader will describe it as `Button`.

Let's add some meaning to these buttons to help people with vision issues understand where they are in the experience. Let's use the `aria-label` attribute on the HTML tag for each of the buttons.

### Before

```jsx
<Masthead
  logo={<OpentextSmallLogo />}
  menuButton={<IconButton flat primary icon={<MenuIcon />} />}
  title={
    <MastheadTitle>
      Primary name
      <MastheadTitleSecondary>Secondary name</MastheadTitleSecondary>
      <MastheadVersion>24.1</MastheadVersion>
    </MastheadTitle>
  }
  actions={[
    <IconButton key="search" flat icon={<SearchIcon />} />,
    <IconButton key="aviator" flat icon={<AviatorPrimaryIcon />} />,
    <IconButton key="notification" flat icon={<NotificationIcon />} />,
    <IconButton key="star" flat icon={<StarIcon />} />,
    <IconButton key="help" flat icon={<HelpCircleIcon />} />,
    <IconButton key="more" flat icon={<MoreCircleIcon />} />,
  ]}
  avatar={<Avatar initials="AA" />}
/>
```

### After

```jsx
<Masthead
  logo={<OpentextSmallLogo />}
  menuButton={<IconButton flat primary icon={<MenuIcon />} aria-label="Open main menu" />}
  title={
    <MastheadTitle>
      <span aria-label="OpenText Product Primary Name">Primary name</span>
      <MastheadTitleSecondary>Secondary name</MastheadTitleSecondary>
      <MastheadVersion>24.1</MastheadVersion>
    </MastheadTitle>
  }
  actions={[
    <IconButton key="search" flat icon={<SearchIcon />} aria-label="Search" />,
    <IconButton key="aviator" flat icon={<AviatorPrimaryIcon />} aria-label="Aviator" />,
    <IconButton key="notification" flat icon={<NotificationIcon />} aria-label="Notifications" />,
    <IconButton key="star" flat icon={<StarIcon />} aria-label="Favourites" />,
    <IconButton key="help" flat icon={<HelpCircleIcon />} aria-label="Help" />,
    <IconButton key="more" flat icon={<MoreCircleIcon />} aria-label="More actions menu" />,
  ]}
  avatar={<Avatar initials="AA" aria-label="User avatar" />}
/>
```

Once added, while performing the same flow as before, the user journey will be more descriptive and discernible for users with vision issues. Adding `aria-label="Open main menu"` makes the purpose of the button clear for screen readers without redundancy. In this case, the screen reader will describee the focus of the menu button as `Open main menu button` - combining the `aria-label` attributes content with the type of element giving a sense of purpose and action which is clear to the user. Moving through the Global header, they will hear the labels for each of the actions.

In future iterations based on final UXD design interaction patterns, you may decide that the `Notifications` button needs to be dynamically updated or animated to show a count of new messages when received by the application. This is where the attribute `aria-live="polite"` combined with `aria-atomic="true"` would come in useful. When `aria-live="polite"` and `aria-atomic="true"` are applied, screen readers announce the full notification count each time it updates. This creates a clear, non-intrusive experience for users.

#### Related reading

- [MDN - ARIA - ARIA Live](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/Accessibility/ARIA/Attributes/aria-live)
- [MDN - CSS - prefers-reduced-motion](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/CSS/@media/prefers-reduced-motion)

Great start, now let's say you need a form in the application.

### Forms

Starting with a basic form layout, we will progressively enhance it by adding ARIA attributes to ensure compatibility with screen readers and keyboard navigation.

The UX Aspects Universal `@ux-aspects-universal/react/form` package contains components that allow you to build functional and usable forms for your applications by providing `form-field`, `required-indicator` and `form-field-feedback` amongst others. But these components on their own still need some implementation inside your application to make them relevant and accessible to everyone.

Forms are often a cause of accessibility problems so let's look at an example of a form built using a range of components that you might find in a product.

On adding the controls to your application, sighted users will see the form contains some required fields denoted by the `*` beside text in the form labels. To a user with vision issues, the screen reader on entering the form might not announce a specific title reducing the context and understanding for the user.

Pressing the `Tab` key the user can navigate the fields but the screen reader would describe the fields as `Name` and `Email address` but would not reflect their required presence in the data that gets submitted.

Let's give some contextual guidance to help people with vision issues understand what they need to fill in and why.

Remember to ensure the form fields are in a logical order in the HTML, as this is the order in which a person using only a keyboard will tab through the fields, and a screen reader will read them to the user.

### Before

```jsx
<Form orientation={orientation}>
  <FormField isValid={status === 'success'}>
    <FormFieldLabel htmlFor="name-input">
      Name <RequiredIndicator />
    </FormFieldLabel>
    <TextInput id="name-input" />
    <FormFieldFeedback icon={icon} status={status}>
      A value is required.
    </FormFieldFeedback>
  </FormField>
  <FormField>
    <FormFieldLabel htmlFor="email-input">
      Email address <RequiredIndicator />
    </FormFieldLabel>
    <TextInput id="email-input" />
  </FormField>
  <FormActionGroup>
    <Button primary>Submit</Button>
    <Button secondary>Reset</Button>
  </FormActionGroup>
</Form>
```

### After

```jsx
<Form orientation={orientation} aria-labelledby="form-title" aria-describedby="form-instructions">
  <h3 id="form-title">User Information</h3>
  <p id="form-instructions">Fields marked with an asterisk (*) are required.</p>

  {/* Name Field */}
  <FormField isValid={status === 'success'}>
    <FormFieldLabel htmlFor="name-input">
      Name <RequiredIndicator aria-hidden="true" />
    </FormFieldLabel>
    <TextInput id="name-input" aria-required="true" aria-describedby="name-feedback" />
    <FormFieldFeedback
      icon={icon}
      status={status}
      id="name-feedback"
      role="alert"
      aria-live="assertive"
    >
      A value is required.
    </FormFieldFeedback>
  </FormField>

  {/* Email Address Field */}
  <FormField>
    <FormFieldLabel htmlFor="email-input">
      Email address <RequiredIndicator aria-hidden="true" />
    </FormFieldLabel>
    <TextInput id="email-input" aria-required="true" aria-describedby="email-feedback" />
    <span id="email-feedback">Enter a valid email address where we can reach you.</span>
  </FormField>

  {/* Action Buttons */}
  <FormActionGroup>
    <Button primary aria-label="Submit form">
      Submit
    </Button>
    <Button secondary aria-label="Reset form">
      Reset
    </Button>
  </FormActionGroup>
</Form>
```

Looking at what was added, you will see some slight differences. First up we have added a heading with an `id` attribute to the form (this is just for this demo purpose as forms may be placed in a `Panel` or `Modal` that includes a title), this `id` can then be referenced by the `aria-labelledby` attribute, in our example it would be by using `aria-labelledby="form-title"`. By adding this attribute to the `form` element we can link the form to a descriptive heading which can then be announced by screen readers to give context to the user.

We have added the ARIA attribute`aria-describedby` to the input fields which can reference multiple elements `id` attributes. You might use this in the scenario that a field has help text to describe a particular format required or for descriptive validation errors that might occur associated with a field. As an example `aria-describedby="email-help email-error"` was added to the `email` input which references the help text but also the error text that might show on submit of the form.

Like the Global header example where we investigated using `aria-live`, we can use that to good effect on forms that announce to users using a screen reader that there are errors they must address before progressing the form. For the email field, we use `uxa-form-field-feedback` with `aria-live="assertive"` to ensure error messages are announced immediately, helping users quickly correct their input.

On the fields that are required to pass validation, you can add the `aria-required="true"` to make it known to users with screen readers in use that the field is required. You can hide elements from the screen reader in certain situations where it would not understand the context on it's own by using `aria-hidden="true"`.

Remember check the documentation for each of the components you are using to show it's internal accessibility input. For example the `Toggle Switch` component activates and changes the boolean value for keyboard users when they press the `Space` key on their keyboard.

#### Related reading

- [Open Text UXD - Forms guideline](https://uxd.opentext.net/guidelines/forms.htm)
- [MDN - ARIA - ARIA Live](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/Accessibility/ARIA/Attributes/aria-live)
- [UX Aspects Universal - Toggle Switch](/react/components/toggle-switch)

You now have an accessible form, built up of multiple form controls that are accessible and functional. After the application sends the values to an API endpoint, you may wish to inform the user that an action is required to proceed in their journey. Let's investigate using a `Message` pattern as per the OpenText Design system guidelines to display and give notice to the user.

### Messages

For this purpose, the UX Aspects Universal provides a messaging pattern component known as `Modal` from the `@ux-aspects-universal/react/dialog` package. We’ll also ensure that the `Modal` is accessible by focusing on ARIA attributes and focus management particularly for users relying on screen readers.

```tsx
import { Button } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/button';
import {
  Dialog,
  DialogContent,
  DialogFooter,
  DialogHeader,
} from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/dialog';
import { SuccessIcon } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/icon/jato-status';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC, useState } from 'react';

const SuccessModalExample: FC = () => {
  const [isOpen, setIsOpen] = useState(false);

  return (
    <>
      <Button primary onClick={() => setIsOpen(true)}>
        Show modal message
      </Button>
      {isOpen && (
        <Dialog icon={<SuccessIcon />} status="success" onClose={() => setIsOpen(false)}>
          <DialogHeader>Success Modal</DialogHeader>
          <DialogContent>This is the message content area.</DialogContent>
          <DialogFooter>
            <Button primary autoFocus onClick={() => setIsOpen(false)}>
              Close
            </Button>
          </DialogFooter>
        </Dialog>
      )}
    </>
  );
};

export default SuccessModalExample;
```

After adding the code within your project, you will see the **Modal message** visible as per the example. With all implementations, they will trap focus within the modal when open. This ensures that users cannot accidentally tab out of the modal, preserving a focused interaction space until they dismiss it. You can test this by triggering your modal to an `open` state so it is visible. `Modal`'s can have action buttons or use a `Button` with the `close` metaphor icon to dismiss the modal. To a sighted user, they would find these patterns and may be familiar with them from other parts of the application or products they use.

For users with vision impairments or those navigating via keyboard, common issues may arise if interactive elements like buttons are not clearly labeled or accessible.

### Before

```jsx
<Dialog icon={<SuccessIcon />} status="success" onClose={() => setIsOpen(false)}>
  <DialogHeader>Success Modal</DialogHeader>
  <DialogContent>This is the message content area.</DialogContent>
  <DialogFooter>
    <Button primary onClick={() => setIsOpen(false)}>
      Close
    </Button>
  </DialogFooter>
</Dialog>
```

### After

```jsx
<Dialog
  icon={<SuccessIcon />}
  status="success"
  onClose={() => setIsOpen(false)}
  role="dialog"
  aria-labelledby="dialog-title"
  aria-describedby="dialog-content"
>
  <DialogHeader id="dialog-title">Success Modal</DialogHeader>
  <DialogContent id="dialog-content">This is the message content area.</DialogContent>
  <DialogFooter>
    <Button primary onClick={() => setIsOpen(false)} aria-label="Close success modal">
      Close
    </Button>
  </DialogFooter>
</Dialog>
```

To address this, lets use the `aria-label` attributes on all actionable elements within the modal, especially those with icons or ambiguous actions. By using the `aria-labelledby` attribute we can link the dialog title to the header and using the `aria-describedby` attribute links the dialog content to the description, so screen readers announce the main content upon opening.

Messages are an important form of feedback which communicate information, status, processes, outcome and errors to the user. They also provide guidance and help focus the user as they work. They can contain other interactive elements such as `Link`, `Button` or form elements and because of this there may be other elements that are focusable within a `Modal`. When launched, the focus will typically be on the first interactive element.

The UX Aspects Universal component follows the ARIA guidelines and provides some level of customization including where focus should land on open, but also allows the `Modal` to be dismissed on press of the `Escape` key on the keyboard or via clicking on the `backdrop` (sometimes called a **Page mask**).

#### Related reading

- [OpenText UXD - Messages guideline](https://uxd.opentext.net/guidelines/messages.htm)
- [W3 WAI-ARIA - Dialog examples](https://www.w3.org/WAI/ARIA/apg/patterns/dialog-modal/examples/dialog/)

## Closing remarks

By now you will have used some of the most common techniques to make accessible products. Ensuring accessibility in every aspect of the experience is crucial to delivering a truly inclusive product. By incorporating ARIA attributes, managing focus effectively, and adhering to best practices, developers and designers together can create an environment where all users can navigate, understand, and interact with ease. Remember, the interactions between keyboard and mouse do not need to be the same but there should be parity in the outcome to give the user a sense of completion.

Accessibility is not a one-time task but a collaborative, ongoing commitment. From initial design choices to final code implementation and testing, every step should prioritize usability for all. By following these guidelines, validating your work against WCAG standards, and embracing user feedback, you will help set a high standard of accessibility in JATO, creating a user experience that empowers every individual.

---

**Universal** was designed from the ground up with dynamic theming at its core. Our goal was to make themes easy to set up, customize, and switch between without requiring app recompilation. Theme switching is optimized to be virtually instantaneous, even allowing specific regions of your applications to use different themes. This foundational concept has been crucial in the development of the **new JATO design system**, enabling us to create multiple design systems concurrently.

## Setting up theming

For JATO we have combined the light and dark themes into a single stylesheet while minimizing the additional bundle size. As a result the process of setting up a theme is simple.

1. **Importing the Theme Stylesheet**: You can import the theme stylesheet by adding the import to your global stylesheet.

### CSS

```css
@import '@ux-aspects-universal/themes/jato/index.css';
```

### SCSS

```css
@use '@ux-aspects-universal/themes/jato/index.css' as *;
```

2. **Setting the Theme**: To specify the default theme, use the `ThemeProvider` around the root component of your application and pass the desired theme.

```tsx
import { ThemeProvider, Theme } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/theming';

render(
  <ThemeProvider theme={Theme.JatoLight}>
    <App />
  </ThemeProvider>
);
```

## Switching themes

You can change the active theme at any time by changing the value passed to the `theme` prop.

```tsx
const [theme, setTheme] = useState<Theme>(Theme.JatoLight);

const toggleTheme = () => {
  setTheme(theme === Theme.JatoLight? Theme.JatoDark : Theme.JatoLight);
};

return (
  <ThemeProvider theme={theme}>
    <App />
  </ThemeProvider>
);
```

---

Design tokens are CSS custom properties (variables) that define the visual attributes of components. They enable consistent, maintainable, and easily customizable themes across your application.

## What are Design Tokens?

Design tokens encapsulate design decisions as named variables. Instead of hardcoding values like `#0073e7` or `16px`, you reference tokens like `var(--uxa-color__primary)` or `var(--uxa-spacing--m)`.

**Benefits:**

- **Consistency**: Uniform visual design across all components
- **Maintainability**: Update values in one place to affect the entire application
- **Theming**: Switch between visual styles without modifying component code
- **Scalability**: Add new themes without recompilation

## Token Categories

### Global Tokens

Global tokens provide foundational values: colors, typography, spacing, elevation etc...

**Examples:**

```scss
--uxa-color__primary                           // Primary brand color
--uxa-typography__font-family                  // Default font family
--uxa-application__background-color            // Default application background color
--uxa-elevation__background-color--1           // Elevation background color
```

### Component Tokens

Component tokens are specific to individual UI components.

**Button Example:**

```scss
// Base properties
--uxa-button__height
--uxa-button__padding-inline
--uxa-button__border-radius

// Primary variant states
--uxa-button__background-color--primary
--uxa-button__background-color--primary-hover
--uxa-button__background-color--primary-disabled
```

This pattern is consistent across all components.

## Naming Convention

Tokens follow a BEM-inspired structure:

```
--uxa-{element}__{property}--{modifier}
```

- `element`: Component name (e.g., `button`, `input`)
- `property`: CSS property (e.g., `color`, `padding`)
- `modifier`: State or variant (e.g., `hover`, `primary`)

> **Note:** Modifiers are ordered by permanence (most to least). For example: `--uxa-button__color--primary-hover` where `primary` (variant) comes before `hover` (state).

**Examples:**

```scss
--uxa-button__background-color                 // Default
--uxa-button__background-color--primary        // Variant
--uxa-button__background-color--primary-hover  // Variant + state
```

## Using Design Tokens

Reference tokens using the `var()` function:

```css
.my-component {
  color: var(--uxa-typography-color__body);
  background-color: var(--uxa-elevation__background-color--1);
  padding: var(--uxa-spacing--m);
}

.my-button {
  background-color: var(--uxa-button__background-color--primary);
}

.my-button:hover {
  background-color: var(--uxa-button__background-color--primary-hover);
}
```

## Overriding Tokens

Tokens can be overriden to give a component a slightly different appearance without having to modify or override the underlying CSS.

### Application-Wide

```css
@import '@ux-aspects-universal/themes/jato/index.css';

:root.uxa-theme-jato-light {
  --uxa-color__primary: #ff6b35;
}
```

### Component-Specific

### CSS

```css
.custom-button {
  --uxa-button__background-color--primary: #ff6b35;
  --uxa-button__border-radius: 0.5rem;
}
```

### Inline Styles

```tsx
<Button
  appearance="primary"
  style={
    {
      '--uxa-button__background-color--primary': '#ff6b35',
      '--uxa-button__border-radius': '0.5rem',
    } as React.CSSProperties
  }
>
  Custom Button
</Button>
```

### Scoped Sections

```tsx
<div className="admin-section">
  <Button primary>Admin</Button>
</div>
```

```css
.admin-section {
  --uxa-color__primary: #9b1e83;
  --uxa-button__border-radius: 0.25rem;
}
```

## Additional resources

The Universal JATO theme includes all the JATO tokens. For more information on the available tokens visit the [Design Tokens](https://cybersecurity-enterprise.glpages.otxlab.net/ux-aspects/jato/design-tokens/#/) website.

For more information on setting up themes, see the [Theming](./theming) guide.

Date time adapters provide a flexible way to customize how UX Aspects Universal components handle dates and times. **You must explicitly configure a date time adapter before using any date or time related components.** You can choose between JavaScript's native `Date` object or more powerful third-party libraries like Luxon or Moment.js.

## Why use a date time adapter?

Date time adapters provide a common abstraction layer that allows UX Aspects Universal components to work with any date and time library without being tightly coupled to a specific implementation. This architectural pattern offers several key benefits:

- **Flexibility**: choose the date library that best fits your application's needs without changing component code.
- **Compatibility**: seamlessly integrate with existing date libraries already used in your application.
- **Future-proofing**: easily switch to newer date libraries (like the upcoming Temporal API) as they become available.
- **Consistent API**: components interact with dates through a unified interface, regardless of the underlying implementation.

While JavaScript's native `Date` object has limitations—particularly with time zones, UTC offsets, parsing/formatting, and internationalization—the adapter pattern's primary advantage is decoupling. This means you can leverage powerful third-party libraries like Luxon or Moment.js for their advanced features, or stick with the native `Date` for simpler use cases, all while using the same component APIs.

## Available adapters

UX Aspects Universal provides three built-in date time adapter implementations. You must configure one of these adapters in your application before using date or time components.

### Native adapter

**Package**: `@ux-aspects-universal/core/date-time`
**Class**: `UxaNativeDateTimeAdapter`
**Date type**: JavaScript `Date`

The native adapter uses JavaScript's built-in `Date` object and requires no external dependencies. However, it has limited functionality:

- ✅ Basic date and time operations
- ✅ No external dependencies
- ✅ Smallest bundle size
- ❌ No timezone support
- ❌ No UTC offset handling
- ❌ Limited internationalization (English only by default)
- ❌ Methods like `getOffset()` and `getOffsetName()` will throw exceptions

The native adapter is suitable for simple applications that don't require advanced date or time features.

### Luxon adapter (recommended)

**Package**: `@ux-aspects-universal/core/date-time-luxon`
**Class**: `UxaLuxonDateTimeAdapter`
**Date type**: `DateTime` (from Luxon)
**External dependency**: [Luxon](https://moment.github.io/luxon/)

Luxon is the recommended adapter if you don't already have a date library preference. It provides:

- ✅ Full timezone support
- ✅ UTC offset handling
- ✅ Comprehensive internationalization
- ✅ Modern API and actively maintained
- ✅ Immutable date objects
- ✅ Powerful parsing and formatting

**Installation**:

```bash
npm install luxon
```

### Moment.js adapter

**Package**: `@ux-aspects-universal/core/date-time-moment`
**Class**: `UxaMomentDateTimeAdapter`
**Date type**: `Moment` (from Moment.js)
**External dependency**: [Moment.js](https://momentjs.com/)

The Moment.js adapter is provided for applications that already use Moment.js. It offers:

- ✅ Full timezone support
- ✅ UTC offset handling
- ✅ Comprehensive internationalization
- ✅ Widely used and familiar API
- ⚠️ Legacy project (in maintenance mode)
- ⚠️ Larger bundle size

**Note**: Moment.js is a [legacy project](https://momentjs.com/docs/#/-project-status/) and should not be used for new applications. Only use this adapter if your application already depends on Moment.js for compatibility with existing code.

**Installation**:

```bash
npm install moment
```

## Setting up a date time adapter

**Important**: You must configure a date time adapter before using any date or time related components. Without an adapter, these components will not function correctly.

### Basic setup

Configure a date time adapter by wrapping your application with `DateTimeAdapterContext.Provider` and passing an instance of your chosen adapter.

#### Luxon example

```tsx
import { UxaLuxonDateTimeAdapter } from '@ux-aspects-universal/core/date-time-luxon';
import { DateTimeAdapterContext } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/date-time';
import { StrictMode } from 'react';
import { createRoot } from 'react-dom/client';
import { App } from './app';

const luxonAdapter = new UxaLuxonDateTimeAdapter();

createRoot(document.getElementById('root')!).render(
  <StrictMode>
    <DateTimeAdapterContext.Provider value={luxonAdapter}>
      <App />
    </DateTimeAdapterContext.Provider>
  </StrictMode>
);
```

#### Moment.js example

```tsx
import { UxaMomentDateTimeAdapter } from '@ux-aspects-universal/core/date-time-moment';
import { DateTimeAdapterContext } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/date-time';
import { StrictMode } from 'react';
import { createRoot } from 'react-dom/client';
import { App } from './app';

const momentAdapter = new UxaMomentDateTimeAdapter();

createRoot(document.getElementById('root')!).render(
  <StrictMode>
    <DateTimeAdapterContext.Provider value={momentAdapter}>
      <App />
    </DateTimeAdapterContext.Provider>
  </StrictMode>
);
```

#### Native adapter example

```tsx
import { UxaNativeDateTimeAdapter } from '@ux-aspects-universal/core/date-time';
import { DateTimeAdapterContext } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/date-time';
import { StrictMode } from 'react';
import { createRoot } from 'react-dom/client';
import { App } from './app';

const nativeAdapter = new UxaNativeDateTimeAdapter();

createRoot(document.getElementById('root')!).render(
  <StrictMode>
    <DateTimeAdapterContext.Provider value={nativeAdapter}>
      <App />
    </DateTimeAdapterContext.Provider>
  </StrictMode>
);
```

### Using the adapter with components

After configuring an adapter, you must pass date objects of the corresponding type to components that accept date or time inputs.

#### With Luxon

```tsx
import { DatePicker } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/date-picker';
import { DateTime } from 'luxon';
import { useState } from 'react';

export function MyComponent() {
  const [selectedDate, setSelectedDate] = useState<DateTime>(DateTime.now());

  return <DatePicker value={selectedDate} onValueChange={setSelectedDate} />;
}
```

#### With Moment.js

```tsx
import { DatePicker } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/date-picker';
import * as moment from 'moment';
import { Moment } from 'moment';
import { useState } from 'react';

export function MyComponent() {
  const [selectedDate, setSelectedDate] = useState<Moment>(moment());

  return <DatePicker value={selectedDate} onValueChange={setSelectedDate} />;
}
```

#### With native Date

```tsx
import { DatePicker } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/date-picker';
import { useState } from 'react';

export function MyComponent() {
  const [selectedDate, setSelectedDate] = useState<Date>(new Date());

  return <DatePicker value={selectedDate} onValueChange={setSelectedDate} />;
}
```

## Internationalization

When using Luxon or Moment.js adapters, you can configure the default locale for date and time formatting by passing options when creating the adapter instance.

### Setting a default locale

Pass an object with the `defaultLocale` property when instantiating the adapter:

```tsx
import { UxaLuxonDateTimeAdapter } from '@ux-aspects-universal/core/date-time-luxon';
import { DateTimeAdapterContext } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/date-time';

const luxonAdapter = new UxaLuxonDateTimeAdapter({ defaultLocale: 'fr-FR' });

createRoot(document.getElementById('root')!).render(
  <DateTimeAdapterContext.Provider value={luxonAdapter}>
    <App />
  </DateTimeAdapterContext.Provider>
);
```

### Changing locale at runtime

You can also change the locale dynamically by calling `setDefaultLocale()` on the adapter instance:

```tsx
import { useDateTimeAdapter } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/date-time';
import { DateTime } from 'luxon';

export function LocaleSwitcher() {
  const dateTimeAdapter = useDateTimeAdapter<DateTime>();

  const handleLocaleChange = (locale: string) => {
    dateTimeAdapter.setDefaultLocale(locale);
  };

  return (
    <div>
      <button onClick={() => handleLocaleChange('en-US')}>English</button>
      <button onClick={() => handleLocaleChange('fr-FR')}>Français</button>
      <button onClick={() => handleLocaleChange('de-DE')}>Deutsch</button>
    </div>
  );
}
```

## Components that use date time adapters

The following UX Aspects Universal components utilize the date time adapter:

- **DatePicker**: Calendar component for selecting dates
- **DateRangePicker**: Component for selecting date ranges
- **TimePicker**: Component for selecting time values
- **TimeOffsetPicker**: Component for selecting UTC offsets

All of these components automatically use the configured date time adapter and expect date values in the corresponding format.

## Creating a custom adapter

You can create custom adapters for other date libraries or specialized requirements by extending the `UxaDateTimeAdapter` abstract class:

```tsx
import { UxaDateTimeAdapter } from '@ux-aspects-universal/core/date-time';

export class MyCustomDateTimeAdapter extends UxaDateTimeAdapter<MyDateType> {
  // Implement all required abstract methods
  getYear(dt: MyDateType): number {
    // Implementation
  }

  getMonth(dt: MyDateType): number {
    // Implementation
  }

  // ... implement all other required methods
}
```

**Note**: New methods may be added to the `UxaDateTimeAdapter` base class in future releases to support additional features. Ensure your custom adapter is kept up to date.

### Extending the native adapter

If you only need to customize localization for the native adapter, you can extend `UxaNativeDateTimeAdapter`:

```tsx
import { UxaNativeDateTimeAdapter } from '@ux-aspects-universal/core/date-time';

export class LocalizedNativeAdapter extends UxaNativeDateTimeAdapter {
  override getMonthNames(): string[] {
    return ['Janvier', 'Février', 'Mars' /* ... */];
  }

  override getWeekDayNames(): string[] {
    return ['Dimanche', 'Lundi', 'Mardi' /* ... */];
  }

  // Override other localization methods as needed
}
```

Then provide your custom adapter:

```tsx
import { DateTimeAdapterContext } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/date-time';
import { LocalizedNativeAdapter } from './localized-native-adapter';

const customAdapter = new LocalizedNativeAdapter();

createRoot(document.getElementById('root')!).render(
  <DateTimeAdapterContext.Provider value={customAdapter}>
    <App />
  </DateTimeAdapterContext.Provider>
);
```

## Best practices

1. **Always configure an adapter**: You must explicitly configure a date time adapter before using any date or time related components
2. **Choose the right adapter**: Use Luxon for new applications, Moment.js only if already in use, and native only for simple use cases
3. **Install dependencies**: Don't forget to install Luxon or Moment.js as a project dependency when using those adapters
4. **Type consistency**: Always use the correct date type (`Date`, `DateTime`, or `Moment`) throughout your application
5. **Configure once**: Set up the date time adapter at the application root level, typically in your main entry file
6. **Locale management**: Configure the default locale when creating the adapter instance for consistent internationalization
7. **Bundle size**: Consider bundle size implications when choosing between adapters
8. **Adapter instance**: Create a single adapter instance and reuse it throughout your application rather than creating new instances

## Migration considerations

If you're migrating from the native adapter to Luxon or Moment.js:

1. Install the required date library package
2. Update your application root to use the new adapter in `DateTimeAdapterContext.Provider`
3. Change all date variable types from `Date` to `DateTime` or `Moment`
4. Update date creation and manipulation code to use the new library's API
5. Test all date-related functionality thoroughly, especially timezone-sensitive features

## Further reading

- [Luxon documentation](https://moment.github.io/luxon/)
- [Moment.js documentation](https://momentjs.com/)

Avatars are a thumbnail representation of an individual, business or entity in the interface.

```tsx
import { Avatar } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/avatar';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const AvatarExample: FC = () => {
  return (
    <div
      style={{
        display: 'flex',
        flexDirection: 'column',
        gap: '16px',
      }}
    >
      <div style={{ display: 'flex', gap: 16, justifyContent: 'center' }}>
        <Avatar initials="A" color="raspberry" size="extra-small" />
        <Avatar initials="A" color="plum" size="extra-small" />
        <Avatar initials="A" color="blue" size="extra-small" />
        <Avatar initials="A" color="cerulean" size="extra-small" />
        <Avatar initials="A" color="teal" size="extra-small" />
        <Avatar initials="A" color="navy" size="extra-small" />
        <Avatar initials="A" color="steel" size="extra-small" />
      </div>

      <div style={{ display: 'flex', gap: 16, justifyContent: 'center' }}>
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="raspberry" size="small" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="plum" size="small" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="blue" size="small" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="cerulean" size="small" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="teal" size="small" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="navy" size="small" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="steel" size="small" />
      </div>

      <div style={{ display: 'flex', gap: 16, justifyContent: 'center' }}>
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="raspberry" size="medium" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="plum" size="medium" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="blue" size="medium" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="cerulean" size="medium" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="teal" size="medium" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="navy" size="medium" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="steel" size="medium" />
      </div>

      <div style={{ display: 'flex', gap: 16, justifyContent: 'center' }}>
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="raspberry" size="large" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="plum" size="large" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="blue" size="large" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="cerulean" size="large" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="teal" size="large" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="navy" size="large" />
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="steel" size="large" />
      </div>
    </div>
  );
};

export default AvatarExample;
```

## Import

Import `Avatar` and `AvatarImage` to use the component.

```ts
import { Avatar, AvatarImage } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/avatar';
```

## API Reference

The following exports are available within `@ux-aspects-universal/react/avatar`:

### Avatar

| Property | Type | Default | Required | Description |
| -------- | ---- | ------- | -------- | ----------- |
| `size` | `'extra-small' | 'small' | 'medium' | 'large'` | - | No | Determine the size of the avatar. |
| `color` | `'raspberry' | 'plum' | 'blue' | 'teal' | 'cerulean' | 'navy' | 'steel'` | - | No | Determine the color of the avatar. |
| `initials` | `string` | - | No | Determine the initials to display. |
| `isBordered` | `boolean` | - | No | Whether the avatar should have a border. |
| `isInteractive` | `boolean` | - | No | Whether the avatar should be interactive. |
| `src` | `string` | - | No | The URL of the image to display. |
| `alt` | `string` | - | No | The alt text for the image. |

## Examples

The following examples demonstrate how to use the Avatar component in different scenarios.

### Avatar with image

Avatars can be used to display an image of the user.

```tsx
import { Avatar } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/avatar';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const AvatarImageExample: FC = () => {
  return (
    <div
      style={{
        display: 'flex',
        gap: 16,
        flexDirection: 'column',
      }}
    >
      <div style={{ display: 'flex', gap: 16, justifyContent: 'center' }}>
        <Avatar size="extra-small" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/18?img=67" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="extra-small" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/18?img=49" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="extra-small" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/18?img=52" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="extra-small" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/18?img=12" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="extra-small" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/18?img=18" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="extra-small" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/18?img=44" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="extra-small" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/18?img=59" alt="Placeholder" />
      </div>

      <div style={{ display: 'flex', gap: 16, justifyContent: 'center' }}>
        <Avatar size="small" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/24?img=67" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="small" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/24?img=49" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="small" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/24?img=52" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="small" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/24?img=12" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="small" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/24?img=18" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="small" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/24?img=44" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="small" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/24?img=59" alt="Placeholder" />
      </div>

      <div style={{ display: 'flex', gap: 16, justifyContent: 'center' }}>
        <Avatar size="medium" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/32?img=67" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="medium" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/32?img=49" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="medium" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/32?img=52" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="medium" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/32?img=12" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="medium" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/32?img=18" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="medium" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/32?img=44" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="medium" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/32?img=59" alt="Placeholder" />
      </div>

      <div style={{ display: 'flex', gap: 16, justifyContent: 'center' }}>
        <Avatar size="large" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/48?img=67" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="large" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/48?img=49" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="large" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/48?img=52" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="large" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/48?img=12" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="large" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/48?img=18" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="large" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/48?img=44" alt="Placeholder" />
        <Avatar size="large" src="https://i.pravatar.cc/48?img=59" alt="Placeholder" />
      </div>
    </div>
  );
};

export default AvatarImageExample;
```

### Avatar with border

Avatars can be displayed with a border to differentiate them from the background.

```tsx
import { Avatar } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/avatar';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const AvatarBorderExample: FC = () => {
  return (
    <div
      style={{
        display: 'flex',
        gap: 16,
        alignItems: 'center',
        justifyContent: 'center',
        backgroundColor: '#efefef',
        padding: 8,
        width: '100%',
        borderRadius: 4,
      }}
    >
      <Avatar
        size="extra-small"
        isBordered
        src="https://i.pravatar.cc/18?img=67"
        alt="Placeholder"
      />
      <Avatar size="small" isBordered src="https://i.pravatar.cc/24?img=67" alt="Placeholder" />
      <Avatar size="medium" isBordered src="https://i.pravatar.cc/32?img=67" alt="Placeholder" />
      <Avatar
        size="large"
        isBordered
        src="https://i.pravatar.cc/48?img=67"
        alt="Placeholder"
      />{' '}
    </div>
  );
};

export default AvatarBorderExample;
```

### Interactive avatar

Avatars may be interactive, allowing users to click on them to perform an action or show a menu.

```tsx
import { Avatar } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/avatar';
import { Menu, MenuItem, useMenuTrigger } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/menu';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const AvatarInteractiveExample: FC = () => {
  const { menuProps, triggerProps } = useMenuTrigger<HTMLDivElement>();
  const { menuProps: imageMenuProps, triggerProps: imageTriggerProps } =
    useMenuTrigger<HTMLImageElement>();

  return (
    <>
      <div
        style={{
          display: 'flex',
          gap: 16,
          padding: 8,
        }}
      >
        <Avatar initials="AA" color="raspberry" size="large" isInteractive {...triggerProps} />

        <Avatar
          size="large"
          isInteractive
          src="https://i.pravatar.cc/48?img=52"
          alt="Placeholder"
          {...imageTriggerProps}
        />
      </div>

      <Menu {...menuProps}>
        <MenuItem>Option 1</MenuItem>
        <MenuItem>Option 2</MenuItem>
        <MenuItem>Option 3</MenuItem>
      </Menu>

      <Menu {...imageMenuProps}>
        <MenuItem>Option 1</MenuItem>
        <MenuItem>Option 2</MenuItem>
        <MenuItem>Option 3</MenuItem>
      </Menu>
    </>
  );
};

export default AvatarInteractiveExample;
```

---

Breadcrumbs are a navigational element that helps users know their current location within a product.

```tsx
import { Breadcrumb, Breadcrumbs } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/breadcrumbs';
import { HomeIcon } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/icon/jato';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const BreadcrumbsExample: FC = () => {
  return (
    <Breadcrumbs>
      <Breadcrumb href="javascript:void(0)" icon={<HomeIcon />} />
      <Breadcrumb href="javascript:void(0)">Breadcrumb 1</Breadcrumb>
      <Breadcrumb href="javascript:void(0)">Breadcrumb 2</Breadcrumb>
      <Breadcrumb href="javascript:void(0)">Breadcrumb 3</Breadcrumb>
      <Breadcrumb href="javascript:void(0)">Breadcrumb 4</Breadcrumb>
      <Breadcrumb>Breadcrumb 5</Breadcrumb>
    </Breadcrumbs>
  );
};

export default BreadcrumbsExample;
```

## Import

Import `Breadcrumbs` to use the component.

```ts
import { Breadcrumbs } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/breadcrumbs';
```

## API Reference

The following exports are available within `@ux-aspects-universal/react/breadcrumbs`:

### Breadcrumbs

The container for the breadcrumbs.

| Property | Type | Default | Required | Description |
| -------- | ---- | ------- | -------- | ----------- |
| `size` | `'small' | 'medium'` | `medium` | No | The size of breadcrumbs. |
| `separator` | `ReactNode | ((index: number) => ReactNode)` | - | No | The separator to use between breadcrumbs. |
| `maxBreadcrumbs` | `number` | - | No | The maximum number of breadcrumbs when responsive is set to true. |
| `isResponsive` | `boolean` | - | No | Whether the breadcrumbs will collapse when there is not enough horizontal space. |

### Breadcrumb

A single breadcrumb item.

| Property | Type | Default | Required | Description |
| -------- | ---- | ------- | -------- | ----------- |
| `href` | `string` | - | No | The URL to navigate to when the breadcrumb is clicked. |
| `icon` | `ReactNode` | - | No | If specified the breadcrumb will be displayed as an icon instead of text. |

### BreadcrumbOverflowButton

A preconfigured icon button that can be used with the `overflowButton` render prop. This button will open the overflow menu when clicked.

## Examples

The following examples demonstrate how to use the Breadcrumbs component in different scenarios.

### Responsive Breadcrumbs

When the available space is too small to display all breadcrumbs, or the `maxBreadcrumbs` property is set, the breadcrumbs can collapse into a dropdown menu.

```tsx
// eslint-disable no-script-url
import { Breadcrumb, Breadcrumbs } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/breadcrumbs';
import { HomeIcon } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/icon/jato';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const ResponsiveBreadcrumbsExample: FC = () => {
  return (
    <div>
      <Breadcrumbs isResponsive maxBreadcrumbs={4}>
        <Breadcrumb href="javascript:void(0)" icon={<HomeIcon />} />
        <Breadcrumb href="javascript:void(0)">Breadcrumb 1</Breadcrumb>
        <Breadcrumb href="javascript:void(0)">Breadcrumb 2</Breadcrumb>
        <Breadcrumb href="javascript:void(0)">Breadcrumb 3</Breadcrumb>
        <Breadcrumb href="javascript:void(0)">Breadcrumb 4</Breadcrumb>
        <Breadcrumb>Breadcrumb 5</Breadcrumb>
      </Breadcrumbs>
    </div>
  );
};

export default ResponsiveBreadcrumbsExample;
```

### Custom Overflow Button

The `overflowButton` render prop can be used to customize or augment the overflow button. A common use case could be to add a tooltip to the button.

```tsx
import {
  Breadcrumb,
  BreadcrumbOverflowButton,
  Breadcrumbs,
} from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/breadcrumbs';
import { HomeIcon } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/icon/jato';
import { Tooltip, useTooltipTrigger } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/popover';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const BreadcrumbsOverflowButtonExample: FC = () => {
  const { tooltipProps, triggerProps } = useTooltipTrigger<HTMLButtonElement>();

  return (
    <div>
      <Breadcrumbs
        isResponsive
        maxBreadcrumbs={4}
        overflowButton={() => <BreadcrumbOverflowButton {...triggerProps} />}
      >
        <Breadcrumb href="javascript:void(0)" icon={<HomeIcon />} />
        <Breadcrumb href="javascript:void(0)">Breadcrumb 1</Breadcrumb>
        <Breadcrumb href="javascript:void(0)">Breadcrumb 2</Breadcrumb>
        <Breadcrumb href="javascript:void(0)">Breadcrumb 3</Breadcrumb>
        <Breadcrumb href="javascript:void(0)">Breadcrumb 4</Breadcrumb>
        <Breadcrumb>Breadcrumb 5</Breadcrumb>
      </Breadcrumbs>

      <Tooltip {...tooltipProps}>View overflowing breadcrumbs</Tooltip>
    </div>
  );
};

export default BreadcrumbsOverflowButtonExample;
```

## Localization

This component uses several strings which must be localized. You can use `BreadcrumbsI18nContext` to configure these strings.

```tsx
import { BreadcrumbsI18nContext } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/breadcrumbs';

<BreadcrumbsI18nContext.Provider value={{ previousButtonAriaLabel: "Menu de débordement du fil d'Ariane", ... }}>
  <Breadcrumbs />
</BreadcrumbsI18nContext.Provider>
```

The following strings are available for localization:

| Name                            | Description                                  | Type     |
| ------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------- | -------- |
| **overflowMenuButtonAriaLabel** | The aria label for the overflow menu button. | `string` |

---

Command (raised) buttons allow users to trigger an action or event with a single click. They help to emphasize call-to-actions (CTAs) in busy layouts. There are two styles of command buttons. A primary button is for the main CTA, or most common action. Secondary buttons are used for other non-primary actions.

```tsx
import { Button } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/button';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const ButtonVariantsExample: FC = () => (
  <div
    style={{
      display: 'flex',
      justifyContent: 'center',
      alignItems: 'center',
      gap: 16,
    }}
  >
    <Button primary>Button</Button>
    <Button secondary>Button</Button>
  </div>
);

export default ButtonVariantsExample;
```

## Import

Import `Button` to use the component.

```ts
import { Button } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/button';
```

## API Reference

The following exports are available within `@ux-aspects-universal/react/button`:

### Button

| Property | Type | Default | Required | Description |
| -------- | ---- | ------- | -------- | ----------- |
| `primary` | `boolean` | - | - | Whether it's a primary button. |
| `secondary` | `boolean` | - | - | Whether it's a secondary button. |
| `size` | `small | medium | large` | - | - | The size of the button. |
| `flat` | `boolean` | - | - | Whether it's a flat button. |
| `isDisabled` | `boolean` | `false` | - | Whether the button is disabled. This prevents user interactions and enables disabled behaviour and styling for buttons. |
| `ariaDisabled` | `boolean` | - | - | Define the aria disabled state of the button. This maintains readability and focusability for screenreaders. |
| `label` | `string` | - | - | In cases where specifying the button content as children is not possible, the label prop can be used. |
| `iconBefore` | `ReactNode` | - | - | The icon placed before the content. |
| `iconAfter` | `ReactNode` | - | - | The icon placed after the content. |
| `href` | `string` | - | - | The URL to navigate to when the button is clicked. If provided, the button will be rendered as an anchor element. |

### useToggle

A hook that can be applied to a button to toggle its state. This is useful for buttons that represent a toggle action.

## Examples

The following examples demonstrate how to use the Button component in different scenarios.

### Variants

The Button component supports two color variants: primary and secondary.

```tsx
import { Button } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/button';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const ButtonVariantsExample: FC = () => (
  <div
    style={{
      display: 'flex',
      justifyContent: 'center',
      alignItems: 'center',
      gap: 16,
    }}
  >
    <Button primary>Button</Button>
    <Button secondary>Button</Button>
  </div>
);

export default ButtonVariantsExample;
```

### Size

The Button component supports three sizes: small, medium, and large.

```tsx
import { Button } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/button';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const ButtonSizesExample: FC = () => (
  <div
    style={{
      display: 'flex',
      justifyContent: 'center',
      alignItems: 'center',
      gap: 16,
    }}
  >
    <Button primary size="small">
      Button
    </Button>

    <Button primary size="medium">
      Button
    </Button>

    <Button primary size="large">
      Button
    </Button>
  </div>
);

export default ButtonSizesExample;
```

### Icons

You can add icons to the Button component using the `uxaButtonIconBefore` and `uxaButtonIconAfter` directives.

```tsx
import { Button } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/button';
import { AddIcon } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/icon/micro-focus';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const ButtonIconsExample: FC = () => (
  <div
    style={{
      display: 'flex',
      justifyContent: 'center',
      alignItems: 'center',
      gap: 16,
    }}
  >
    <Button primary iconBefore={<AddIcon />}>
      Button
    </Button>

    <Button secondary iconAfter={<AddIcon />}>
      Button
    </Button>
  </div>
);

export default ButtonIconsExample;
```

### Subtle Buttons

The subtle (flat) button is a variation of the command (raised) button without a background color. Subtle buttons are used for lightweight, non-critical actions, such as within components, and to provide local actions. All the variants and sizes are available.

```tsx
import { Button } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/button';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const SubtleButtonVariantsExample: FC = () => (
  <Button primary flat>
    Button
  </Button>
);

export default SubtleButtonVariantsExample;
```

### Link Buttons

The Button component can be used as a link by setting the `href` prop.

```tsx
import { Button } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/button';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const ButtonLinksExample: FC = () => (
  <div
    style={{
      display: 'flex',
      justifyContent: 'center',
      alignItems: 'center',
      gap: 16,
    }}
  >
    <Button primary href="javascript:void(0)">
      Button
    </Button>
    <Button secondary href="javascript:void(0)">
      Button
    </Button>
  </div>
);

export default ButtonLinksExample;
```

### Toggle Button

The Button component can be used as a toggle button by using the `useToggle` hook. This allows the button to maintain a pressed state.

```tsx
import { Button, useToggle } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/button';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const ButtonToggleExample: FC = () => {
  const { toggleProps } = useToggle();

  return (
    <div style={{ display: 'flex', justifyContent: 'center', alignItems: 'center' }}>
      <Button flat secondary {...toggleProps}>
        Button
      </Button>
    </div>
  );
};

export default ButtonToggleExample;
```

---

Cards are flexible containers that show high-level information, representing a single subject or entity, and can guide the user towards related actions or details.

```tsx
import { Button, IconButton } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/button';
import {
  Card,
  CardChipList,
  CardContent,
  CardFooter,
  CardHeader,
  CardSubtitle,
  CardTitle,
} from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/card';
import { Chip } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/chip';
import { ImageIcon, MoreHorizontalIcon } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/icon/micro-focus';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const BasicCardExample: FC = () => {
  return (
    <Card tabIndex={0} style={{ maxWidth: 370 }}>
      <CardHeader avatar={<img src="./assets/card-avatar.png" alt="card avatar" />}>
        <CardTitle>Title</CardTitle>
        <CardSubtitle>Subtitle</CardSubtitle>
      </CardHeader>

      <CardChipList>
        <Chip size="medium">Status</Chip>
        <Chip size="medium">Value</Chip>
        <Chip size="medium">Value</Chip>
        <Chip size="medium">+9</Chip>
      </CardChipList>

      <CardContent>
        Use content hierarchy within the card to attract your user's attention to the most important
        information.
      </CardContent>

      <CardContent size="small">Text Content</CardContent>

      <CardFooter
        leading={
          <>
            <IconButton secondary flat size="small" icon={<ImageIcon />} aria-label="Action" />
            <IconButton secondary flat size="small" icon={<ImageIcon />} aria-label="Action" />
            <IconButton
              secondary
              flat
              size="small"
              icon={<MoreHorizontalIcon />}
              aria-label="Action"
            />
          </>
        }
        trailing={
          <>
            <Button secondary flat size="small">
              Button
            </Button>
            <Button secondary flat size="small">
              Button
            </Button>
          </>
        }
      />
    </Card>
  );
};

export default BasicCardExample;
```

## Import

Import the card components to use them in your application.

```ts
import {
  Card,
  CardHeader,
  CardTitle,
  CardSubtitle,
  CardFooter,
  CardContent,
  CardChipList,
} from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/card';
```

## API Reference

The following exports are available within `@ux-aspects-universal/react/card`:

### Card

The `Card` component is a container for grouping the card content.

| Property | Type | Default | Required | Description |
| -------- | ---- | ------- | -------- | ----------- |
| `image` | `ReactElement` | - | No | The image to display at the top of the card. Provide this to utilize the preview card layout. |

### CardHeader

The `CardHeader` is a container for grouping the card header content, such as the title, subtitle, avatar and actions.

| Property | Type | Default | Required | Description |
| -------- | ---- | ------- | -------- | ----------- |
| `avatar` | `ReactElement` | - | No | The avatar to display in the card header. |
| `thumbnail` | `ReactElement` | - | No | The thumbnail to display in the card header. |

### CardTitle

The `CardTitle` is a container for displaying the card title.

### CardSubtitle

The `CardSubtitle` is a container for displaying the card subtitle.

### CardContent

The `CardContent` is a container for grouping the card content.

### CardChipList

The `CardChipList` is a container for grouping the chips within the card.

### CardFooter

The `CardFooter` is a container for grouping the card footer content.

| Property | Type | Default | Required | Description |
| -------- | ---- | ------- | -------- | ----------- |
| `isStacked` | `boolean` | `false` | No | Whether the footer content should be stacked. |
| `leading` | `ReactNode` | - | No | The leading content to display in the footer. |
| `trailing` | `ReactNode` | - | No | The trailing content to display in the footer. |

## Examples

The following examples demonstrate how to use the Card component in different scenarios.

### Thumbnail Card

The Thumbnail Card is a variation of the Card component that includes a thumbnail image.

```tsx
import { Button, IconButton } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/button';
import {
  Card,
  CardChipList,
  CardContent,
  CardFooter,
  CardHeader,
  CardSubtitle,
  CardTitle,
} from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/card';
import { Chip } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/chip';
import { ImageIcon, MoreHorizontalIcon } from '@ux-aspects-universal/react/icon/micro-focus';
import * as React from 'react';
import { FC } from 'react';

const ThumbnailCardExample: FC = () => {
  return (
    <Card tabIndex={0} style={{ maxWidth: 370 }}>
      <CardHeader thumbnail={<img src="./assets/card-thumbnail.svg" alt="card thumbnail" />}>
        <CardTitle>Title</CardTitle>
        <CardSubtitle>Subtitle</CardSubtitle>
      </CardHeader>

      <CardChipList>
        <Chip size="medium">Status</Chip>
        <Chip size="medium">Value</Chip>
        <Chip size="medium">Value</Chip>
        <Chip size="medium">+9</Chip>
      </CardChipList>

      <CardContent>
        Use content hierarchy within the card to attract your user's attention to the most important
        information.
      </CardContent>

      <CardContent size="small">Text Content</CardContent>

      <CardFooter
        leading={
          <>
            <IconButton secondary flat size="small" icon={<ImageIcon />} aria-label="Action" />
            <IconButton secondary flat size="small" icon={<ImageIcon />} aria-label="Action" />
            <IconButton
              secondary
              flat
              size="small"
              icon={<MoreHorizontalIcon />}
              aria-label="Action"
            />
          </>
        }
        trailing={
          <>
            <Button secondary flat size="small">
              Button
            </Button>
            <Button secondary flat size="small">
              Button
            </Button>
          </>
        }
      />
    </Card>
  );
};

export default ThumbnailCardExample;
```

### Preview Card
